@@139084674 <p> Dr. Chilton received the National Medal of Technology &amp; Innovation from President Biden in 2023 . Jacquelyn Martin/Associated Press <p> When Mary-Dell Chilton , then known as Mary-Dell Matchett , enrolled at the University of Illinois in the 1950s , women were discouraged from pursuing careers in science . That did n't stop her from planning to major in physics , but falling asleep in the dull freshman lectures did . <p> She also considered astronomy , but was told by a professor that she could not take courses on the subject until her sophomore year . <p> " The hell with that , " she recalled thinking , in a 2008 interview with Scientific American . <p> Instead , she chose chemistry . She would go on to become a pioneering figure in agricultural biotechnology , leading the research team recognized for creating the first genetically modified plant , in 1982 -- a discovery that would transform global agriculture . <p> Dr. Chilton and her colleagues developed a method for inserting the genes of a foreign organism into a plant , which would eventually result in higher-yielding crops @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Get Starting Point <p> A guide through the most important stories of the morning , delivered Monday through Friday . <p> Enter Email <p> At her retirement party in 2018 , her son Mark Chilton later said , " I had everyone raise a glass to the astronomy professor who turned her away . " <p> Mary-Dell Chilton died on June 24 at her home in Carrboro , N.C. , near Chapel Hill . She was 87 . The cause was congestive heart failure , Mark Chilton said . <p> " She was truly driven by the thought that the world needed to have the best science could offer in order to help humanity feed itself , " Andrew Binns , an emeritus professor of biology at the University of Pennsylvania who collaborated with Dr. Chilton on developing the first genetically modified plant , said in an interview . <p> Among other prestigious awards , Dr. Chilton received the World Food Prize , likened to a Nobel Prize for food and agriculture , in 2013 . Ten years later , she received the National Medal of Technology and Innovation @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have Dr. Chilton to thank for protecting their crops from disease , pests , and climate shocks , " Tom Vilsack , the CEO of the World Food Prize Foundation and a former US secretary of agriculture , said in a tribute after her death . <p> Roughly 90 percent of soybeans , cotton , corn , and sugar beets grown in the United States are now genetically modified . While most scientists agree that engineered foods are safe to eat , public opinion remains polarized . Questions have been raised about the long-term effects on human health and the environment ; how many genetically modified crops actually address the concerns of climate change ; the dangers of corporate monopolies on seed supplies ; and the extent to which the promised higher crop yields have been realized . <p> For her part , Dr. Chilton defended genetically modified food , noting that plant engineering had been happening in nature for centuries . <p> " If people understood the science , I think the concern would evaporate , " she said in 2016 in an interview with the alumni association of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's degree in chemistry in 1960 and her doctorate in 1967 . <p> After doing postdoctoral research in bacterial genetics at the University of Washington in Seattle , Dr. Chilton joined the faculty there in 1970 , one of only two women in the department of microbiology and immunology . She worked with a soil-borne microbe called Agrobacterium tumefaciens , a kind of Uber driver for gene transport . In time , she would come to be known as the Queen of Agrobacterium . <p> As part of a class assignment in the mid-1970s , a student presented a paper by a Belgian scientist who proposed that Agrobacterium could insert its own DNA into a plant cell , causing plant cancer -- tumor-like growths called crown gall disease . Dr. Chilton was skeptical . <p> " I was in it to debunk the whole story , " she said in a 2016 oral history for the Genetic Engineering and Society Center at North Carolina State University . <p> In the end , she was happy to prove herself wrong . <p> In 1977 , Dr. Chilton and her collaborators published @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ transfer a piece of its own DNA into the cells of a tobacco plant . The DNA then merged with the plant 's chromosomes , causing the plant to produce tumors and nutrients that ensured the bacterium 's survival . It was a natural genetic engineer . <p> " We could scarcely believe our eyes , " Dr. Chilton wrote in 2017 in a biographical essay , " My Secret Life , " published in The Annual Review of Plant Biology . <p> The possibilities for manipulating plants were enticing . Dr. Chilton was 38 at the time , married with two young sons , and had no path to tenure at the University of Washington . In 1979 , she and her family moved to St. Louis , where she joined the biology faculty at Washington University . <p> Continuing to work with Agrobacterium , she led a research team that showed it was possible to disarm the tumor-causing genes and use the bacterium to transfer foreign genes of choice into a plant cell . In 1982 , her team , working with Binns , transferred a yeast gene @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the gene was passed on to the plant 's descendants . <p> It was effectively the birth of the technology for genetically engineering plants . It paved the way for implanting genes in other crops , including corn , cotton , and soybeans , to produce desirable traits -- among them , resistance to pests and herbicides . <p> That success and similar achievements by a corporate competitor , Monsanto , and scientists from Belgium and Germany were announced at a symposium in Miami in January 1983 . Dr. Chilton 's accomplishment was also acknowledged three months later in the journal Cell . <p> " It became pretty clear that this was going to have huge implications with crops , with agriculture , " Binns said . <p> Mary Dell Matchett II was born Feb. 2 , 1939 , in Indianapolis and named after her mother , Mary Dell ( Hayes ) Matchett , who ran the household . ( Her first name was hyphenated after a teacher called her Mary , which her mother disliked . ) Her father , William E. Matchett , was an insurance executive . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ teenager , she lived mostly with her maternal grandparents in Southern Pines , N.C. , because an older brother tormented her , she wrote in her autobiographical essay . Her grandmother Henrietta Dell Hayes , who owned a clothing store and kept her own books , was a formative influence on Mary-Dell , her son said , showing her " that women can do things in the world . " <p> She finally rejoined her immediate family , who had moved to suburban Chicago , and attended high school there . She built a telescope and , in 1956 , was one of eight girls among the 40 national finalists in a prestigious Westinghouse science competition . She received a National Merit Scholarship to attend the University of Illinois . <p> In 1966 , she married Scott Chilton , who worked as a professor of chemistry , biology , and botany during his career . He died in 2004 . In addition to her son Mark , she leaves another son , Andrew , and two grandchildren . <p> Dr. Chilton left academia in 1983 and returned to North Carolina @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ now Syngenta , a global agribusiness and biotech company , where she worked on the genetic engineering of corn and cotton , among other projects . <p> She acknowledged being flattered by the praise for her achievements . " I 'm an iconic character , " she jokingly told The Raleigh News &amp; Observer in 2013 . <p> " You ca n't stop me , " she said upon being inducted into the National Inventors Hall of Fame in 2015 . " When I 'm after something , I work on it endlessly until I get it . " 
@@139084762 <h> Climate crisis : 2026 could become the second warmest year on record , with scientists warning that crossing the 1.5 ? C threshold is becoming easier . <p> Around half of 2026 is still to come . This means that any answer to the question posed above is likely to be uncertain . Answering this question with any degree of certainty is also best left to climate scientists , who are better equipped to handle it . However , a layman 's understanding of the possible answer -- this year can easily rank second warmest , although being the warmest is not impossible -- can help us understand some important things about the climate crisis . <p> 2026 may rank among the hottest years ever recorded as global temperatures remain close to the critical 1.5 ? C warming threshold . ( AP Photo ) <p> The short summary of the climate crisis implications of the possible answer is as follows . The first thing the possible answer shows is that breaching the 1.5 ? C threshold in particular months does not necessarily need an El Nino . The second @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ easy even in the annual average . The third thing it shows is the corollary of the first two : does the rank of the year matter when no special circumstances are needed for breaching the 1.5 ? C threshold ? The long answer is as follows . <p> The reason why checking possible warming scenarios for 2026 is interesting at all is because of what has come before . The past years -- 2023 , 2024 , 2025 -- were the warmest on record ; and are ranked the second warmest , the warmest , and the third warmest , respectively . Moreover , while only 2025 breached the 1.5 ? C threshold -- scientists expect catastrophic changes in climate if long-term warming relative to the pre-industrial baseline breaches this threshold -- both 2023 and 2025 were very close to the threshold . These two years recorded 1.48 ? C and 1.47 ? C warming , and the average warming in the 2023-2025 period is 1.52 ? C. In other words , 2026 will confirm if 1.5 ? C is going to continue as the new normal . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the spectre of 2026 possibly being the warmest-ever year . The first half of 2026 is quite warm , too . The average temperature for the year up to July 14 ( the latest available data from the ERA5 dataset published by the Copernicus Climate Change Service ( C3S ) ) is 1.45 ? C warmer than the pre-industrial average . This is the third-highest average for this part of the year , behind 2024 and 2025 . <p> With past trends summarised , one can now check how much warming is required in the rest of the year for 2026 to breach particular thresholds . To maintain the third rank 2026 holds for the year up to July 14 , the rest of the year needs to average 1.50 ? C. Similarly , the rest of the year needs to average 1.53 ? C , 1.56 ? C , and 1.77 ? C , respectively , to be ranked second warmest , to breach the 1.5 ? C threshold at the end of the year , and to be ranked the warmest ever . <p> Warming in the 1.50-1.56 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the rest of 2026 . Here is why . 2026 hit the 1.50 ? C threshold in February and recorded 1.47 ? C and 1.48 ? C warming in January and February despite La Nina-like conditions being in place in November and December . La Nina is a periodic cooling of the surface of the central-eastern equatorial Pacific that is expected to cool global temperatures with a 2-4 month lag . That temperatures were still around 1.50 ? C warmer than normal suggests the baseline warming in the world is teetering on the edge of that threshold for at least the cooler months of the year ( long-term warming has so far been relatively higher in the northern hemisphere 's cooler months at the beginning and end of the year than in other months ) . <p> If baseline warming of the cooler months is close to 1.5 ? C , the rest of the year should not have a problem recording warming in the 1.50-1.56 ? C range because La Nina 's warmer counterpart -- El Nino , which warms global temperatures -- was in place in May . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in May , the July 15-December 31 period averaged 1.68 ? C warming . <p> gfx <p> To be sure , it is possible that El Nino 's impact will be seen in global temperatures with the usual lag of two-four months , but it seems to have already eroded any lingering impact of La Nina conditions . While months up to April were ranked fourth or fifth warmest , both May and June this year were the second warmest on record . Clearly , the only question is whether El Nino 's impact will be strong enough for the rest of the year to average the 1.77 ? C warming required for the year to be the warmest ever . That is a tall order . Only two months in human history have breached the 1.77 ? C threshold : December 2023 and February 2024 , which recorded 1.78 ? C and 1.77 ? C warming . However , it may not be impossible . Scientists widely expect this year 's El Nino to mature into one of the strongest ever . 
@@139090668 <p> Orange-hued skies , wildfire smoke and scorched forest land have been regular occurrences across North America in recent years as rising global temperatures contribute to massive forest fires . Many of those are in northern Canada , home to some of the world 's largest intact forests . <p> Canada currently has more active wildfires than it did at the same stage in either of the past two years , while the area burned so far this year exceeds the 10-year average . <h> Size , intensity of ordinary wildfires grow <p> Canada , the world 's second-largest country in area , has nearly a tenth of the world 's total forest area and about 24% of the world 's boreal forests . Many of them are unreachable by road and uninhabited . <p> Forest fires are a natural process that kill pests and clear out unhealthy shrubbery . But in recent years , the size and intensity of fires have increased . <p> Rising temperatures due to climate change mean drier forests . Drier forests allow fire to spread faster . Fires are often started by lightning or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Netherlands Carillon experience the smoke from Canadian wildfires as it clouds the Washington skyline as seen from Arlington , Virginia , US , July 17 , 2026. ( credit : REUTERS/JONATHAN ERNST ) <p> Mike Flannigan , a professor of wildland fire at Thompson Rivers University in British Columbia , noted the area burned in Canada has almost quadrupled since the 1970s . Warmer temperatures have prolonged the wildfire season , and more lightning is also causing fires , he said . <p> Climate scientist Andrew Dessler , a professor of atmospheric sciences at Texas A&amp;M College of Arts and Sciences , said the Canadian fires were burning in largely unmanaged forests where , for the last one hundred years , they had been allowed to burn until they died out . <p> Like Flannigan , he said higher temperatures were fueling bigger , more intense blazes in Canada . <p> " The reason this is having a big effect on the U.S. right now is because of climate change ; that 's the thing that has changed . Nothing else has changed , " he said . <p> As @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ smoke follows the wind . Fires in northern and western Ontario as well as northern Minnesota brought the world 's worst air quality to Toronto earlier this week , then spread to New York and Washington . Detroit and Chicago by Friday had the world 's worst air quality . <h> Governments try to protect communities <p> While some fires are unavoidable , governments are trying to protect communities by closing forest areas to prevent human-caused fires , using non-flammable building materials in high-risk locations and being better prepared . <p> The federal government has allocated more money to fight wildfires , including C$316.7 million ( $227 million ) for aerial firefighting capacity over five years and C$47.8 million for the Parks Canada National Fire Management Program . The Ontario government spent C$271 million on emergency firefighting in 2025-26 , exceeding its budget of C$135 million . It has allocated C$150 million for 2026-27 . <p> After Canada 's biggest wildfire season by area burned in 2023 , experts and politicians began calling for a national response organization . Those calls intensified after fires in 2024 engulfed a third of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ only Group of Seven country without a federal agency focused on combating wildfires . Much of the firefighting responsibility falls to provinces . <p> In June 2026 , the Canadian Senate published a report that called for a federal coordinating office for wildfires and emergency response , and proposed funding a national fleet of modern firefighting aircraft , among other measures . <p> " Wildfires are now a crisis , " the report said , noting the record area burnt in recent years . <p> The federal Office of Emergency Management and Community Resilience said it was considering recommendations on wildfire response and the possible creation of a federal emergency management agency . It cited the leasing of 10 new firefighting aircraft as an example of how it is addressing severe wildfires by bolstering provincial and territorial firefighting capacity . <p> Canada currently has roughly 126,000 firefighters who work for towns , villages and cities , of which about 90,000 are volunteers . Only 3,000 to 5,000 firefighters are trained to tackle wildland fires across the country , said Ken McMullen , the president of the Canadian Association of Fire @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ training and moving firefighters and equipment across the country when needed , McMullen said . 
@@139090669 <h> " This is not a distant threat to health , " Prof. Asaph Aharoni told the Post , saying up to two-thirds of the world 's population already lacks essential micronutrients . <p> The world has been worrying that climate change will make it more difficult to raise crops to feed mankind . But little attention has been given to " hidden hunger , " in which people have plenty to eat of carbohydrates that can even cause obesity but lack vital vitamins and minerals . <p> Climate change is speeding up the decline in important nutrients in our food , according to 15 leading researchers from four continents -- including Prof. Asaph Aharoni of the Institute of Science 's plant and environmental sciences department at the Weizmann Institute of Science in Rehovot . <p> " This is not a distant threat to health , " he told The Jerusalem Post in an interview . " Already today , up to two-thirds of the world 's population does not get enough essential micronutrients -- including folic acid , vitamin B2 , calcium , iron , and iodine -- from @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hunger , " he added . <p> In addition , more than 700 million people suffer from actual hunger , lacking sufficient calories to meet their basic nutritional needs . To confront this growing global threat , the prominent scientists issued an urgent call to enhance the nutritional quality of the world 's food supply . <p> The Weizmann Institute of Science in Rehovot. ( credit : Wikimedia Commons ) <p> The initiative that included Aharoni was led by Prof. Dominique Van Der Straeten of Ghent University in Belgium and Prof. Alisdair Fernie of the Max Planck Institute of Molecular Plant Physiology in Germany . <p> In a comprehensive review published in the prestigious journal Nature under the title " Genetic technologies to enhance crop nutritional value under climate change , " the researchers presented a roadmap for protecting and improving the nutritional quality of crops in the face of climate change . <p> They advocated the use of advanced genetic technologies -- including CRISPR gene editing -- to increase the vitamin and mineral content of crops while also making them more resilient to a changing climate . <p> CRISPR-Cas @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has emerged as a groundbreaking breeding technology that has already been adopted by many countries . <p> The team studied CRISPR-Cas-based approaches that could be used to achieve biofortification targets by enhancing micronutrient densities to the levels necessary to reduce dietary vitamin and mineral deficiencies . <p> Given the limited time frame available to achieve zero hunger , they argued that CRISPR-Cas technologies should be combined with metabolic engineering based on transformation and other technologies , they stressed . <p> The researchers also considered untapped resources beyond metabolic pathways and current CRISPR-Cas techniques to deal with one of the most important societal issues of the 21st century . <p> From an agricultural viewpoint , they named three major objectives that need to be worked towards simultaneously to achieve zero hunger : enhanced yield , higher vitamin and mineral density to sustain recommended daily intake , and enhanced climate-change resilience . <p> Although the Green Revolution increased global calorie production , it worsened hidden hunger by giving priority to higher yields over nutritional quality . Stress from global climate change has been shown to reduce the densities of several micronutrients . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the agriculture faculty of the Hebrew University of Jerusalem in Rehovot , completed his doctorate at the University of Wageningen , the Netherlands , which is recognized as the world 's leading institution for agricultural and forestry sciences -- driving innovation in sustainable food systems , plant sciences , and the environment . <p> He has launched eight startups in the agriculture field . <h> Who this will affect <p> The study is very timely , said Aharoni , because just a few weeks ago , the European Union formally approved new regulations for gene-edited crops , referred to as New Genomic Techniques ( NGTs ) . This marks a major shift from strict , traditional genetically modified organism ( GMO ) rules . <p> The legislation divides gene-edited plants into two categories , streamlining the approval process for crops with targeted edits that could occur naturally . <p> The first category includes plants featuring limited genetic edits ( such as those that could occur through conventional breeding or mutation ) . They will no longer be subjected to the strict regulations of traditional GMOs , and once verified , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ do not require special labeling . <p> The second category includes plants with more complex genetic modifications that will remain subject to the EU 's existing , stringent GMO legislation and safety assessments . They updated the regulations to help farmers adapt to climate change and reduce pesticide reliance by cultivating pest- and drought-resistant crops . <p> " Minerals and vitamins , " said Aharoni , " could be added using CRISPR and genetic engineering , or only with genetic engineering . Drought is the main climate-change event that reduces the levels of vitamins and minerals inside crops , but higher carbon dioxide and heat can also be factors . If current trends continue and the action that we recommended is not taken , there will be a lot of hidden hunger . " <p> People in poor countries will be affected . " So will children who do n't get enough vitamin A , pregnant women who lack folic acid , and large groups that do n't get enough vitamin D , which is n't well absorbed from pills or drops but is better absorbed from food , " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are boosted nutritionally and approved , they could be imported or grown in Israel and available in supermarkets in the next three to five years . <p> If he had unlimited funding , the first research project he would carry out would be genetic engineering of all vitamins . <p> " We have a lot of ideas , but because of antipathy to Israel around the world , it 's harder to get grants or cooperation with other research institutes , " he said . " The EU has placed sanctions on us , and consortia in Belgium or Spain , for example , do n't want to include us . " <p> Many in the general public still do n't believe that the climate is changing for the worse and will affect the growth of crops and their nutritional value . " We need increased funding to persuade them , " Aharoni concluded . " We have to raise their awareness . " <p> The Environment and Climate Change portal is produced in cooperation with the Goldman Sonnenfeldt School of Sustainability and Climate Change at Ben-Gurion University of the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to the content .

@@139115369 <p> Amid predictions of a " super " El Ni ? o ahead , expectations are high that San Diego 's coastline could get walloped again starting in the late fall . <p> Warnings of extreme storms and resultant flooding , erosion and widespread damage abound , along with growing lists of how to prepare for coming turbulent weather . <p> There may be some reason for long-term optimism in reports by the Scripps Institution of Oceanography focused on El Ni ? os . <p> One published July 8 in the journal Science Advances carried the headline " Targeted marine cloud brightening could weaken subsequent El Ni ? o . " In essence , the idea is to create shade -- or dim sun in the Pacific , if you will -- and reflect back some sunlight that , in theory , could tamp down the higher ocean temperatures that lead to the extreme El Ni ? o conditions . <p> Late last year , two other studies were issued , the San Diego Beach Report : 2025 and a companion study in Nature Communications that found local beaches recovered @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , even surprising one of the study 's authors . <p> El Ni ? o is a climate occurrence in which the surface waters of the central and eastern Pacific Ocean become unusually warm , increasing moisture in the atmosphere . Its counterpoint , La Ni ? a , is a cooling of surface ocean waters and drier weather . <p> The concept of marine cloud brightening , or MCB as researchers call it , is not new . Some previous thinking held that this needed to take place on a large scale -- such as shooting aerosols from planes high in the stratosphere -- and would require intricate global cooperation . <p> Scripps researchers looked at the possibility of using this geoengineering over a shorter term in a relatively regional application that shortens the timeline and reduces the bureaucratic complexity of implementing it . <p> This is still largely uncharted territory that generates a lot of questions and concerns about unintended consequences , though perhaps not as much in a more targeted approach . <p> The technique for MCB entails spraying seawater into marine clouds to enhance the clouds @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ controlled trials have tested the technique 's efficacy , they 've only been on very small scales , " said a Scripps summary of the study . <p> Ironically , a fire disaster fueled by hot , dry weather , not damaging rain , was key to the research . The " Black Summer " bushfires that scorched Australia in 2019 and 2020 served as a " natural experiment , " according to the study . <p> " The smoke that wafted into the atmosphere was filled with reflective , cloud interacting aerosols akin to those used in a geoengineering method called marine cloud brightening , " according to the report . <p> Other scientists found the smoke-filled clouds bounced back solar radiation into space , enhancing the La Ni ? a that formed in 2020 . <p> " It was the key breakthrough to this becoming a viable research question . Without that validation opportunity , I do n't think our findings would be so credible , " said Scripps Oceanography researcher Kate Ricke , who co-authored the study with Jessica Wan , a researcher at Scripps who has @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Relatively quick , targeted marine cloud brightening intervention contrasts with other geoengineering efforts to address human-caused global warming that take international coordination over years or decades , Ricke and Wan said . <p> Meanwhile , there 's more direct heartening news for beach areas in San Diego and across the state . The annual report found most of the beaches studied in the region " grew in width last year as beaches entered a post-El Ni ? o recovery phase , " according to Scripps . <p> The other study in Nature Communications discovered that California 's " average beach width has remained remarkably stable across nearly four decades despite notable examples of beach erosion . " <p> " Being able to look across multiple years of the beach report lets managers assess trends , which can help inform decisions about potential beach nourishment projects , " said Mark Merrifield , director of the Scripps Center for Climate Change Impacts and Adaptation and one of the co-authors of the beach report . <p> " On the flip side , our research also establishes that multi-year cycles of widening or narrowing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ often occur after a strong erosion year . " <p> Adam Young , another co-author , underscored that the findings were significant because beaches are the first defense against coastal flooding , in addition to providing recreational , cultural and economic benefits to the region . <p> The report 's authors also said some of the recent increase in beach width was due to beach nourishment projects in Encinitas and Solana Beach . The sand added for each of those projects has been generally migrating south . <p> " The sense that the system is n't necessarily losing sand as fast as we might have predicted was surprising , " said Merrifield , according to Axios San Diego . " It brings a slightly different perspective on long-term planning . " <p> Researchers could not attribute increased or decreased beach erosion to higher global average temperatures . " However , the report 's authors said such a trend might emerge if accurate records of beach widths extended farther back in time , " according to Scripps . <p> In the near term , however , that moisture-packed climate phenomenon , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ last week 's fair-weather king tide flooding in Mission Beach seem quaint . <p> Copyright 2026 San Diego Union-Tribune . @@139161761 <h> Why Study English ? <p> In the fall of 1980 , I joined the Department of English at Emory University as a new assistant professor . Then , as now , jobs for Ph.D.s in English were scarce , and then , as now , to get one you had to be good and lucky . The good part was in my control . I had a handful of publications , six years ' experience teaching writing and literature , and one of my degrees was from Yale -- always eye-catching for members of a search committee . But none of that would have mattered if a job in my field , theater history , had not opened unexpectedly that year . <p> Emory 's undergraduate curriculum in English was then typical of most departments of that era ; faculty taught courses in Chaucer , Shakespeare , Milton , the Romantic poets and William Faulkner ; in British and American authors from the 19th and 20th centuries ; and in poetry and prose from the Middle Ages , the Renaissance and the Victorian and modern eras . I apologized to the young man for my rudeness , then asked everybody to search " ovenbird " on their smartphones . It was hugely satisfying to see 15 young men and women discover that Frost was n't being elliptical or obscure . There really is a creature called an ovenbird ( scientific name Seiurus aurocapilla ) , a migratory species of small warbler common to mid- and northeast America . <p> It 's a small , indistinctive bird , and there 's not much reason to write an entire poem about it @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is one of only a very few species of American songbirds that sing not just during the spring mating season but continue trilling into the hot summer months . The ovenbird 's strident , repetitive call can be heard in deciduous forests all through July and August when most other birdsongs have vanished . Its persistence in warbling after springtime is gone , as well as its peculiar , seemingly instructive call -- guidebooks and websites say the ovenbird seems to be chanting " tee-chur , tee-chur " -- made the ovenbird perfect for the characteristically melancholy Frost to use in framing a lesson about life . Here 's the poem in full : <p> There is a singer everyone has heard , Loud , a mid-summer and a mid-wood bird , Who makes the solid tree trunks sound again.He says that leaves are old and that for flowersMid-summer is to spring as one to ten.He says the early petal-fall is pastWhen pear and cherry bloom went down in showersOn sunny days a moment overcast ; And comes that other fall we name the fall.He says the highway dust is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that he knows in singing not to singThe question that he frames in all but wordsIs what to make of a diminished thing . <p> Frost 's poem is straightforward : He simply described a real bird and a practical lesson to be learned from it in the most concise and appealing way he knew . " The Oven Bird " is emphatically instructive , and it ought to be read and taught accordingly . You do n't have to have a rural , New England upbringing to make sense of the poem and to learn from it . It 's especially relevant to a room of 20-year olds who might use it to begin a serious conversation about the springtime of their own lives , so to speak , now recently past , and to ponder how best to spend the " diminished thing " of adulthood . <p> It may sound simpleminded to ask students to read Frost for the moral rather than , say , to critique his sexism ( as does Joyce Carol Oates in her short story " Lovely , Dark , Deep " ) @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ land was ours before we were the land 's " ) , but lyricism is itself a mode of learning . <p> At the end of Aristophanes ' comic drama Frogs ( 405 BCE ) , the character Euripides gives the audience two reasons to value poets . Nothing in Euripides ' speech would have sounded controversial to Aristophanes ' audience ; on the contrary , being told that poets were teachers would have seemed unsurprising , probably even superfluous . One imagines them responding like a crowd of voters at a Trump rally , murmuring some Attic equivalent to " Right on ! " And why would they not have thought that ? " <p> Opening up the literary canon has too often led students to a disingenuous relativism among authors and literary works . @@139172977 <p> Before 2023 , the only comparable smoke events in NYC history were more than 100 years ago , during the Adirondack fires of 1903 and 1908 . It 's worth asking why we were blessed with such a long smoke-free interval , and why we now are entering a " new normal " with negative impacts on public health and mortality . <p> The 1903 and 1908 fires also followed prolonged droughts , but were localized in the Adirondacks and ignited not by lightning but by sparks from wood- and coal-burning trains and a farmer 's intentional burning of a fallow field . They were made worse by piles of highly combustible slash left by loggers . The New York State government passed reform policies that stopped those fires from becoming recurring events . <p> In contrast , many of the current wildfires in Canada , and those in 2023 , began in remote areas with no road access and were ignited by lightning strikes . More than two million acres have burned thus far this year , 90% in areas that have never been " managed . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ recent report from the U.S. National Academy of Sciences , indicate that human-induced climate warming played a major role . <p> Our carbon emissions have heated the planet nearly 1.5 degrees Celsius . In Canada , temperature increases have been about twice that global average , and nearly three times as high in northern Canada . The smoke-filled air New York City experienced last week and three years ago is not the result of local circumstances like it was in the early 1900s . It 's a symptom of a global problem . <p> Adapting to the presence of unhealthy levels of smoke in ambient air means mostly staying indoors with the windows closed , using high-quality indoor air purifiers , and wearing a properly fitted N95 or KN95 mask outside . The NYC government helped make those masks available to residents and the state government has promoted the use of portable HEPA air cleaners and provided guidance for clean-air centers . <p> We should be ready for more wildfire smoke in our future . While NYC emergency management services do a laudable job of monitoring and reporting on air @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 's list of priority health risks due to climate change , where it belongs alongside heat waves , flooding , and the power outages that both of those can cause . <p> Although the 2026 Canadian wildfires will likely be linked to the " super " El-Ni ? o we are entering , we are a large part of the problem . American carbon emissions per capita are nearly three times the global average and are increasing following a brief decline . If we continue to burn billions of tons of fossil carbon each year , we should n't expect a different result . Plants took millions of years to remove that carbon from the Earth 's ancient atmosphere . We 're reversing that geological process in the blink of an eye . <p> We do n't need to stay on this self-destructive course . The global revolution in renewable energy technology has made this a choice , not an inevitability . <p> Last week , billions of sports fans wondered how the smoke would affect the World Cup final for the first time in its history . @@139205963 <p> A drone image of a southern right whale mother floating on her back , her calf nearby . <p> Fredrik Christiansen <p> Join Our Community of Science Lovers ! <p> Enter your email <p> I agree my information will be processed in accordance with the Scientific American and Springer Nature Limited Privacy Policy . We leverage third party services to both verify and deliver email . By providing your email address , you also consent to having the email address shared with third parties for those purposes . <p> Being a mom can be an exhausting job . Taking a break every now and then is much needed for mothers of many species -- whales included . And that could be the motivation behind a peculiar habit of some southern right whales ( Eubalaena australis ) , the only whale species known to flip upside down . <p> " This is definitely an unusual behavior , " says Renae van Noort , a conservation biologist at the University of Western Australia . " It looks quite odd , especially when you see them from a distance with their pectoral @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ whale mothers live extreme lives . They spend months eating and storing energy in Antarctica and then embark on long migrations to reach their calving grounds , where they give birth and care for their young -- all without eating for months . At this crucial time , resting and saving energy becomes a matter of life or death because they need enough to both feed their young and make the journey back to their feeding grounds , where they will finally be able to eat again . <h> On supporting science journalism <p> If you 're enjoying this article , consider supporting our award-winning journalism by subscribing . By purchasing a subscription you are helping to ensure the future of impactful stories about the discoveries and ideas shaping our world today . <p> Van Noort and her team decided to take a closer look at how these whale mothers conserve energy by filming them and their calves using drones in the bay of Head of the Bight in South Australia , where southern right whales aggregate and give birth . Some 75 hours of footage of 90 mother-calf pairs @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of their time resting , the team reported on July 6 in Mammalian Biology . Calves also rested , mostly when their mothers were resting , too . <p> The cetaceans most commonly rested by floating dorsal-side-up at the surface , as well as below it . But some mothers -- 25 percent of the total -- also had another strategy : upside-down resting . " We are still unsure why some mothers rest this way and others do n't , " van Noort says . <p> The behavior only occurs in southern right whales . This may be because mothers of other species have different ways of conserving energy , says Malin Lilley , a comparative psychologist at Texas A&amp;M University , who was n't involved in the new study . " Or they might not rest in habitats that are as relatively undisturbed by human activity as this particular population of southern right whales , " she adds . Rolling on their backs makes whales more vulnerable to being hit by ships . <p> Mothers rolled onto their backs only rarely -- just 2 percent of their time @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ team documented the behavior only in lactating mothers and one pregnant whale , which suggests that getting in this position might be nothing more than a way for whale moms to get a break . When they are lying upside down , their calves can not reach the mammary slits to get milk -- meaning the mothers can decide when it 's feeding time . <p> Lying upside down might also help with regulating their temperature , cooling it off thanks to the fresh air hitting their fins . <p> Lilley says that future research could investigate how and when whale mothers learn to rest in this position . " They might learn from their own mothers , from previous experiences with calves or maybe from observing fellow mothers , " she says . <p> Gennaro Tomma is a freelance journalist who covers science , with a focus on the natural world , biodiversity , conservation , climate change , environmental and science-related policies , and more . His new novel , Ghost-Eye , attempts to do that . Unfortunately , Rubsam argues , it fails at its task . But when I read through the comments on his article , I saw readers arguing that plenty of writing does address global overheating , and expressing the opinion that the genre known as " climate fiction " was n't getting its due . <p> They 're right -- lots of genre fiction deals with apocalyptic climate disruptions . Similarly , I do n't need fiction to describe how dangerous a flash flood is , or what it 's like for a tornado to rip through your town . I 've experienced those things myself , and they 're certain to recur in my lifespan . I want my fiction to be bolder : to use language and imagination that bring me to a new kind of comprehension , not just a re-creation of what I already know . <h> What to Read <p> I can now say that I 've been reading Baker for more than 20 years , or more than half my life . Caroline Blumberg/AP Photo/Caroline Blumberg <p> ARCACHON , France ( AP ) -- Large forest fires burned out of control in southwestern France and central Spain on Friday , prompting authorities to evacuate an estimated 92,000 people from their homes . " <p> More than 20,000 people had already been evacuated from towns and vacation resorts on Thursday . @@139255470 <h> For fisherwomen , rising temperatures are only one part of a larger crisis . Pollution , ecological degradation , and changing seas are making livelihoods increasingly precarious <p> A fisherwoman sells her catch at a market in Chennai Photo Credit : M. Srinath <p> A minute is always 60 seconds . But it rarely feels that way for Kavitha , 40 , selling fish under an unforgiving sun . <p> By seven each morning , she has to arrange the day 's catch neatly on a small table at Chennai 's bustling Nochikuppam fish market , one of the city 's fishing hamlets tucked between the Marina Loop Road and the Bay of Bengal . Buyers drift in , bargain , leave , and return . <p> " Between each sale , I keep an eye on the fish . As the mornings are getting hotter , I worry about selling everything before the sun spoils their freshness , " she says . <p> Rising temperatures are not the only challenge fisherwomen face . Changes in the sea itself have made earning a livelihood far more difficult . Kavitha @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ work around the predictable rhythms of two fishing seasons : vaadai , characterised by northerly winds and currents , and kachan , marked by southerly winds and calmer seas . But those rhythms are no longer as dependable . <h> Changing sea , shrinking catch <p> According to S. Palayam , veteran fisherman from Urur Kuppam , Chennai , the traditionally calmer kachan season has become , on average , 33 days shorter than it once was , while the vaadai season now lingers well beyond its usual withdrawal . <p> His study , ' Sea Changed ; Seasons Changed : A Fisher Science View of Climate Change ' , which draws on daily observations recorded over 1,179 days between September 2018 and October 2023 , also found an increase in rough sea days during kachan , disruptions in the productive transition between the two seasons , and the disappearance in recent years of vanda thanni -- a nutrient-rich phenomenon that traditionally brought abundant fish closer to shore . <p> Fisherwomen sense these changes not through measurements of wind speeds or currents , but through the uncertainty they bring to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , poor catches or fewer fishing days , reduces the fish available for sale , thereby shrinking women 's daily earnings and , with it , the financial agency they exercise within their families . <p> " My husband manages the bigger expenses and repays our loans . But the money I earn gives me some independence . I can buy groceries , pay for my son 's school needs , or spend on small things without having to ask anyone , " says Kavitha . <p> Fishers say the waters close to the shore no longer yield the catch they once did . Forced to travel farther into the sea , they incur higher fuel costs and spend longer hours fishing , even as their returns continue to shrink . " We 're spending ? 5,000 to ? 7,000 more per month , but the catch is much less , " says Kavitha . <p> Although overfishing by mechanised trawlers remains the biggest reason for declining catches , many fisherwomen say changing weather and sea conditions have made their work increasingly uncertain . The seasonal cues they once relied @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ seas , and unpredictable ocean currents have made it increasingly difficult to anticipate fish availability and plan fishing trips as they did in the past . <h> Pollution deepens woes <p> S. Velvizhi , marine biologist at the M.S. Swaminathan Research Foundation ( MSSRF ) , says climate change is only one of several pressures bearing down on marine fisheries . Marine pollution , overfishing and the degradation of critical habitats such as coral reefs , seagrass beds and river estuaries have all contributed to the depletion of fish stocks , she says . <p> In Pulicat , India 's second-largest brackish water lagoon that straddles Andhra Pradesh and Tamil Nadu , fishers say declining catches are also linked to the periodic closure of the lagoon 's sea mouths , which regulate the exchange of water with the Bay of Bengal . When these remain closed , water flow into the lagoon is restricted , salinity rises during the summer months , and fish populations decline . <p> Standing knee-deep in the waters of the Pulicat lake in Tiruvallur district , Sarala , 50 , spends hours hand-picking prawns to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ over the years . Decades of industrial pollution have degraded the lagoon 's ecosystem , while invasive species believed to have been introduced through ballast water have further disrupted native fish populations . <p> Sarala recalls earning ? 200 to ? 300 after two hours of prawn picking ; today , she says , four hours in the lagoon fetches her only about ? 150 . <p> The shrinking income has consequences beyond the day 's meals , Sarala says . " I use it to look after my elderly parents , pay my monthly seetu chit contribution , and save whatever little I can for my daughter . Earlier , I could manage all that . Now , after meeting everyday expenses , there 's hardly anything left to save . " <h> Making ends meet <p> Like many fisherwomen in Tamil Nadu , Selvi , 50 , a resident of Pattipulam in Chengalpattu , says she survives by borrowing . As declining fish catches have reduced her daily earnings , she has found it increasingly difficult to meet household expenses , let alone repay old debts . She @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ borrowing from one lender to repay another and to keep her fish vending business going . " I keep rotating the loans , " she says . " If the catch is poor , there is n't enough money to repay them . So I take another loan . That 's how we 've been managing . Even when benefits reach a family , they usually come through the male fisher . At first , she learnt simply to sew for herself , her mother , and her sister . Encouraged by her younger sister , she now hopes to turn it into an additional source of income . <p> " My younger sister keeps telling me , ' Why do n't you take orders from others ? You stitch well . ' " She has not yet begun taking customers but sees tailoring as a way to cushion her family 's income on days when there is little fish to sell . " If I can get more orders , I 'll continue doing it . At least there will be some income even when business at the fish market is poor @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ value-added products such as dried fish and pickles or small self-help group enterprises . These supplementary incomes help soften the blow , but women say they rarely compensate for the steady earnings that fish once brought . " A few of us get together to make small batches of fish pickle , which we sell through a neighbourhood grocery shop , " says Sarala . <p> Velvizhi says many fishing villages have already demonstrated that alternative livelihood models can work when women receive the right support . " There 's much more of an entrepreneurial mindset among fisherwomen today . Earlier , when we suggested making value-added products like fish pickles or marketing them through WhatsApp , many were hesitant . Now , they 're far more open to trying new ideas , " she says . <p> But adapting to a changing fisheries sector , she says , requires more than encouraging women to diversify . @@139285061 <p> MONTROSE , Colo . ( AP ) -- As water shot into an open stock tank atop a high country mesa , a trickle soaked the dusty earth below . @@139305362 <p> There are solutions , though , that could bring immediate relief . Getting there requires honest conversations and political will from our elected officials . Effective approaches all require using penalties andfees -- or call some what they truly are , taxes -- to reduce traffic by reducing the number of cars on the road , especially at peak hours . <p> Taxes are unpopular , but it always bears repeating : If you 're driving into Boston you 're going to pay . It 's just a question of whether you pay with your time , or your money . <p> Taxes serve two purposes : to raise revenue and incentivize behavior . The US government gives a tax break for charitable donations and home mortgages to encourage philanthropic giving and community stability . Higher car taxes would be a way to raise revenue to improve our transportation system and unclog our roads . <p> Congestion pricing would also be trickier to do in Boston . Manhattan is a grid , so charging people to drive south of 60th Street , which is largely a commercial area , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is often interspersed with commerce , where do you draw the lines ? What if a resident living in downtown Boston wants to go shopping outside of the congestion-pricing zone then reenters ? Should they be charged ? <p> These are problems that can be smoothed out , but it would take time and buy in from different levels of government . In New York City , Mayor Michael Bloomberg proposed congestion pricing legislation way back in 2007 . The plan was finally enacted into law in 2019 , then implemented six years later -- a mere 18 years after proposed . <p> In the here and now , Massachusetts ' elected officials have " a toolbox " of options , said Terrance Regan , a lecturer in transportation planning at Boston University . " You 're taking a scalpel rather than a hammer to some of these problems . " <p> One idea is dynamic pricing : charge more to drive into Boston on the Pike during rush hour , for example . That would be quick and easy . The Massachusetts Department of Transportation Board of Directors could @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ could also target truck traffic , penalizing deliveries during peak hours or encouraging them overnight . New York City 's Department of Transportation began providing financial incentives for businesses to shift deliveries between 7 p.m. and 6 a.m . In London , FedEx and UPS were vocal opponents of that city 's congestion pricing scheme , which began in 2003 , until they realized they could navigate the streets more quickly delivering packages that had to arrive during the workday . <p> An easier calculation would be to simply raise excise taxes or car registration fees . <p> These ideas would be unpopular and also punish all drivers , including those in rural parts of the state where traffic is n't a problem . But it 's also worth stating the obvious : The cost of using our roads has never been free . Taxpayers fund construction and maintenance , we just do n't think about it . Beyond time lost in congestion , driving pollutes our air , contributing to respiratory problems and climate change . <p> For too long , Massachusetts ' elected officials have done nothing for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tax " has long been a non starter for politicians of all stripes . Former governor Charlie Baker rejected congestion pricing , calling it " unfair " for drivers . Governor Maura Healey chastised her former transportation secretary for even floating the idea of new tolls . <p> Good tax policy also bakes in incentives . Under London 's congestion pricing scheme , which quickly dropped traffic by 30 percent , it costs about $24 to drive downtown , but that money has been funneled into improving and expanding bus service . <p> Elected officials have a host of options in the short and long terms . It 's well past time to make some choices . We can quibble about the best path forward , but we should all accept that something needs to change . <p> Or we can continue with the status quo , do nothing , and complain about being stuck in traffic .

In the tank , solids become liquids , sludge drops to the bottom and a layer of scum rises to the top . It operates when nearly full , with each flush pushing out water into a drainfield through a series of long , perforated pipes in a trench surrounded by gravel or other porous filler . Dirty water slowly seeps out of those holes into the soil . At that point , the earth takes over , essentially filtering out the bacteria and viruses before they come in contact with groundwater below . <p> COMPROMISED : Climate change is pushing groundwater closer to the surface more often near the coast because of heavy rains and pressures from rising sea levels . @@139307070 <h> Public health improvements stall amid Trump 's DEI crackdown <p> 6 hours , 22 minutes ago by Briah Lumpkins / KFF Health News <p> Earl Williams , former community development director with the Thomasville Community Development Corp. , stands in front of a building the Georgia city hoped would be turned into a healthcare clinic in the Dewey City neighborhood . " <p> A $19.8 million Community Change Grant from the Environmental Protection Agency was supposed to shift the tides of the neighborhood Williams grew up in . <h> Photo Gallery <p> But three months after winning the grant , Thomasville became one of 80 communities across the country -- from Hawai'i to Alabama to Massachusetts -- to see the funding stripped as priorities shifted in Donald Trump 's second presidency . <p> In an emailed statement , EPA spokesperson Mike Bastasch said the administration is " fully committed to being a great steward of taxpayer dollars . " He added that the " radical agenda " of DEI and environmental justice " preferencing " are not within the agency 's core mission of protecting human health and the environment . <p> Environmental health advocates argue , though , that the decision to cut the grants was not rooted in science . They say that equating environmental justice to DEI is an act of bad faith @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ long-overdue updates needed to protect public health for marginalized populations . <p> " I unfortunately believe we 're going to see a reversal in the health profile of this country , " said Jalonne White-Newsome , an associate professor at the University of Michigan who served in the Biden administration as the first federal chief environmental justice officer . <p> " This current federal regime has decided that humanity and public health and justice do n't mean anything , " she said . <p> ' WHY US ? " <p> Those areas have a deep history : Dewey City , for example , was the first neighborhood in the city where Blacks could build homes after the Civil War . " But it 's worth bringing it back online . " <p> The proposed " Restoring Resiliency " project featured a plan to remove toxic substances such as mold , lead paint and asbestos from homes ; repair a nearly 82-year-old wastewater system , and convert part of a shuttered high school band room into a health clinic . <p> " This was a long and storied attempt to bend the arc of justice closer , " said Jeremy Rich , a Thomas County commissioner and the pastor at First Missionary Baptist Church . ( REUTERS/Diego Vara ) <p> July 27 -- For insurers , the periodic El Ni ? o climate phenomenon has traditionally lowered the risk of costly Atlantic hurricanes , but decades of population growth along coastal areas and rising property values are forcing them to rethink ? that assumption . <p> Marked by warmer-than-average sea surface temperatures in parts of the Pacific Ocean , El Ni ? o typically suppresses Atlantic hurricanes by creating atmospheric conditions that make it harder for storms to form . <p> U.S. government scientists said El Ni ? o arrived in June , which was also the start of the Atlantic hurricane season . They expect a below-average season in 2026 of eight to 14 named ? storms and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ would be good news for property insurers , which in recent years have faced an average of $30 billion in insured losses each season , that is less and less the case , said insurance brokers , catastrophe modelers and analysts . <p> Decades ? of development along U.S. coastlines have dramatically increased the amount of property exposed to hurricanes , with ? the population of coastal counties rising by more than 40 million since 1970 , federal data shows . <p> Combined ? with higher home values and reconstruction costs , both of which have risen more than 70% and 60% , respectively , over the past decade , that coastal expansion means that even a relatively quiet hurricane season can produce ? severe losses . <p> As a result , insurers are rethinking how they ? assess and price catastrophe risk , focusing less on the number of storms and more on where they make landfall and the value of property in their path , ? executives said . <p> " All it takes is one landfalling hurricane to create an insured loss of a magnitude we 've @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in an El Ni ? o year , " said Kimberly Roberts , advisory leader of North American peril at reinsurance broker Guy Carpenter . <p> WHERE STORMS HIT MATTERS MOST <p> A hurricane season generates on average 14 named storms , including seven hurricanes , of which three are major , according to government data , with annual insured losses averaging around $30 billion between 2016 and 2024 , according to data from Aon . <p> Insured losses across El Ni ? o cycles , which occur every two to seven years , are not directly comparable because the phenomenon has no set calendar period , lasting anywhere from nine to 15 months . <p> Since 1950 , El Ni ? o cycles have on average produced about two fewer named storms , according to Jeffrey Strong , senior scientist for tropical cyclone modeling at insurance data firm Verisk . <p> But those numbers mask the true risks . The 1992 ? season , which came on the tail end of the 1991 El Ni ? o , for example , generated roughly half the average number of storms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , one of the strongest storms ever to hit the United States , making landfall in South Florida and again in Louisiana . <p> Andrew alone was so devastating that it would cost the insurance industry nearly $100 billion if it struck today , according to the Swiss Re Institute . <p> In contrast , the record season of 2020 , a non-El Ni ? o year with 30 named storms , 14 hurricanes and seven major hurricanes , generated around $30 billion of insurance losses , mostly because they did not hit populous areas , the Institute said . <p> " Hurricanes are like real estate : the three most important things are location , location , location , " it added . <p> INSURERS BUILDING MORE SOPHISTICATED MODELS <p> El Ni ? o is also associated with above-average rainfall across parts of the southern United States , increasing the likelihood of flooding , landslides and related damage -- ? an adjacent risk insurers also have to increasingly consider . <p> " We 're seeing higher insured values , more concentration in coastal areas , and more complex supply @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ today can be materially higher than what we saw even a decade ? ago , " said Monica ? Ningen , CEO of Property &amp; Casualty Reinsurance US at ? Swiss Re . <p> But El Ni ? o is only one piece of an increasingly complex climate puzzle for catastrophe risk insurers . <p> While hurricanes were once their chief catastrophe risk , climate change is increasing flooding and wildfires , in turn making risk modeling more intricate and challenging . <p> " History has a limit in terms of how much it can teach us , " said Steve Bowen , chief science officer at reinsurance broker Gallagher Re , noting that the changing weather patterns could be reducing the predictive value of risk models . <p> Insurers are responding by developing increasingly sophisticated catastrophe models that combine seasonal climate signals with property-level exposure data to estimate the range of financial losses they could face from natural ? disasters . <p> Some risk experts say insurers should also focus more ? on longer-term trends that are reshaping catastrophe risk , such as the rising costs of reconstructing properties @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ losses . <p> The industry is also ? embracing artificial intelligence to improve risk models . AI ? can help insurers analyze larger volumes of weather and exposure data , said Myra Thomas , an analyst at eMarketer , noting , however , that it can " only go so far . "

@@139331369 <h> Inside the wildfire crisis raging across France and Spain Explained <h> The latest wildfire emergency extends beyond France and Spain ; Italy evacuated hundreds of people from its Adriatic coast as separate fires swept through Puglia , while Scotland and other Mediterranean countries have also reported significant flare-ups . <p> Firefighters battle a forest fire in Saint-Jean-d'Illac , some 30km from Bordeaux , on July 26 , 2026 as a wildfire has been ravaging the forest north of the Arcachon Basin ( Gironde ) . Photo Credit : AFP <p> The story so far : Southwestern Europe is witnessing one of its worst wildfire emergencies in decades . Since late July , untamed fires in France 's Gironde department and across three provinces west of Spain 's Madrid have driven over 325,000 people from their homes . <p> Both the countries have been dealing with dozens of minor wildfires amid deadly heatwaves circulating through the continent . France 's fire broke out near Saumos in the M ? doc on July 22 and tore toward L ? ge-Cap-Ferret , forcing evacuation of the entire Arcachon Bay area . By @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . Gironde 's prefecture had ordered 220,000 people out , and crews lit deliberate " backfires " near Marcheprime as a last defensive line . In Spain , regional authorities suspect a blaze that started near Burgohondo , in Avila 's Sierra de Gredos , around July 20-21 , swept through the Valle de Iruelas nature reserve , merged with a fire in Madrid and reached into Toledo province . By July 26 , its perimeter measured some 160 km , with nearly 89,000 people evacuated . <h> Wildfires amid Europe 's summer heatwave <p> Roughly 69,500 hectares in France have been lost to wildfires since January , according to the EU 's EFFIS satellite system . Spain 's toll stands near 150,000 hectares for the same period , with the Avila-Madrid-Toledo fire alone damaging 77,000 hectares , according to the country 's Environment Minister . <p> France has deployed about 1,000 firefighters , a military aircraft carrying 20,000 litres of retardant , and planes loaned by Croatia , Portugal , Czech Republic , and Slovakia . Spain has mobilised over 2,700 personnel , including a military emergency unit , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ through the EU 's civil-protection mechanism . Both crises coincide with Europe 's ongoing third summer heatwave . An earlier spell led to roughly 1,000 excess deaths in France in June . <p> A firefighter helicopter dumping water is pictured as a fire started in an industrial area of San Martin de Valdeiglesias , about 70km west of Madrid , on July 26 , 2026 . Photo Credit : AFP <h> Growing influence of climate change <p> For the first time on French soil , the Gironde fire generated a pyrocumulonimbus : a storm cloud born of the fire 's own superheated air plume , which cools , condenses ash-laden moisture , and triggers lightning that ignites new blazes , authorities said . <p> Scientists say the crisis reflects the growing influence of climate change on Mediterranean fire regimes . Higher temperatures accelerate evaporation , cause vegetation to dry quickly and lengthen fire seasons . Frontline workers have pointed out that these conditions and the change in wind patterns have further complicated suppression efforts . <h> Emergency responses <p> Governments in both nations have been escalating their responses . Spain @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Toledo and Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez is pressing Opposition parties to back a climate pact on prevention funding and crisis communication . French President Emmanuel Macron has convened an emergency Cabinet meeting on Monday ( July 27 , 2026 ) . Authorities continue to issue mandatory evacuation orders and have urged tourists to avoid affected regions , particularly around Bordeaux and the Gironde coast . <p> Local media reported the death of two French firefighters during their line of duty near Bordeaux . A now-contained fire near Almer ? a killed thirteen and left ten missing earlier this month , Spain 's deadliest in years , while one more person died near Valencia . Losses include destroyed homes , closed motorways and disrupted rail lines . Culturally and ecologically , Bordeaux 's surrounding vineyards -- Gironde alone holds some 115,000 hectares of vines , France 's top wine-growing department -- sit in the fire 's projected path , while most parts of Avila 's Valle de Iruelas reserve , home to one of Europe 's most important black-vulture colonies , and Castellon 's Sierra de Espadan natural park have already @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Spain . Italy has evacuated hundreds of people from its Adriatic coast as separate fires swept through Puglia , while Scotland and other Mediterranean countries have also reported significant flare-ups . <p> Evacuees in both countries have been told that they can not return home until the fires are brought under control .
