@@139359066 <h> The Return of the Monarch <p> " ? Mire ! ? " My guide , Silvestre , pointed upward , as if I was n't already walking with my neck craned as far back as it could go . The sky was alive with millions of fluttering shapes , congregating into clouds at random , occasionally turning into ribbons that wound through the trees . So many butterfly wings beat the air that their vibrations were audible , like a waterfall rushing in the distance . I understood for the first time the immensity of the event I 'd come to see . <h> Explore the September 2026 Issue <p> I was in the center of El Rosario butterfly sanctuary , part of Mexico 's Monarch Butterfly Biosphere Reserve . Unaccustomed to the thin air at nearly 11,000 feet above sea level , I 'd ridden Pelucha , a rust-colored mare , to the heights of the reserve . About halfway up , the orange-and-black butterflies had begun appearing in groups , alighting on flowers or gathering to sip water from puddles . Coupled pairs mated everywhere , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ avoid stepping on them ; Pelucha happily crunched more than a few underfoot . I dismounted at the top of the trail and followed Silvestre as he ducked under a rope that kept tourists from accessing the most sensitive areas of the reserve . <p> It was February , and the monarchs -- northerners that had wintered in Mexico -- were preparing for their final journey , which doubled as the first leg of a multigenerational relay race north . Their delicateness belies a life cycle that more resembles long-haul trucking than anything you might read in a children 's book . Each of the butterflies above me had already braved many grueling trials . In the following week or so , the majority would be on their way . <p> Monarch migration is a wonder of the natural world , one of the longest annual cycles of movement undertaken by any creature on Earth , spanning thousands of miles . Over the past half century , the lands that the monarchs traverse have become more hostile to them , a result of development and herbicides , global warming and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of that migration in doubt . And yet , the story I 'd come to write -- my reason for interloping in this and other sanctuaries -- was not just a narrative of beauty being diminished by human trampling and greed . Recently , there 's been good news : Population estimates , after a long march downward , have been increasing for the past two years . This stabilization appears to be connected at least in part to efforts to save butterfly habitats in Mexico , the United States , and Canada . Those developments are reason to hope that there may still be a pathway left for saving the monarchs and their migration . <p> The monarch persists partly because of the dogged work from its human partners ; because of people who have given their life -- in every sense of the phrase -- to preserve nature . But mostly , the monarch persists because life 's job is to live ; because within those thimble-size bodies hides a dynamo of survival that puts our most incredible machines to shame . The more we learn about the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that they have so far beaten the odds we have stacked against them , and how forbidding those odds remain . <p> We 've known just how epic the monarch 's migration is for only about half a century . In the 1970s , using paper tags placed on butterflies ' wings , the Canadian butterfly researchers Fred and Norah Urquhart completed a lifelong mission to uncover the mystery of where all the monarchs born in Ontario went in the fall . To their surprise , the sojourners they followed made it all the way down the length of the U.S. , and on to the mountain forests of Michoac ? n , Mexico . There they roosted , packed tightly into just a handful of colonies that collectively cover no more than a few city blocks , yet hold nearly all of the continent 's monarchs in winter . The Urquharts first visited these colonies in 1976 , and National Geographic published a cover story on their breakthrough . <p> For centuries , Indigenous people in Mexico 's highlands have witnessed the monarchs ' annual return during harvest season @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ know where they came from . The butterflies have been making this migration for as long as 20 millennia , once finding paths to and from the fields gouged out by glaciers retreating with the spring during the last Ice Age . Now their roosting grounds sit within a handful of sanctuaries that I visited . At Sierra Chincua , a few miles north of El Rosario , I stood on a cliff overlooking the slender trunks of oyamel fir trees . I watched living streams of monarchs flying in different directions , sometimes winding about one another like a gigantic knot forever being tied , and looked in vain for three monarchs that I knew were in the fray . <p> Each had been given a designation in a spreadsheet -- CHI016 , CHI013 , and CHI001 -- but those were all very boring names for butterflies , so I 'd asked my 6-year-old daughter to give them better ones . She 'd come up with Cinderella , Eric , and Carle . ( She knows The Very Hungry Caterpillar by heart . ) <p> Nothing about the trio @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Affixed to each butterfly 's thorax , like a tiny backpack , was a transmitter the size of a grain of rice . These transmitters , called BluMorpho tags , had been developed by a company called Cellular Tracking Technologies , and Cinderella , Eric , and Carle were part of the first generation of monarchs to wear them . <p> Before my visit to the sanctuaries , I 'd spoken with David La Puma , an executive at CTT and one of the brains behind the BluMorpho . The company had initially focused on developing tech for tracking eagles , but La Puma had previously worked in monarch conservation , and carried his interest in the butterflies with him . Tracking a monarch is different from tracking an eagle , as monarchs each weigh less than a dollar bill . Any transmitter attached to one could weigh no more than a few grains of sand . <p> No battery could be made that small and still provide power over months of flying , and the solar panels that CTT used for eagle trackers were much too heavy . But @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ solar squares that line the wings of satellites and carefully chopping them up into tiny bespoke cells . An early version of the tracker , applied to butterflies with dabs of eyelash glue , was linked with a cellphone app via Bluetooth , allowing enthusiasts to follow nearby butterflies -- a sort of " Pok ? mon Go for monarchs , " La Puma said -- and record their locations in somewhat haphazard fashion . The newest iteration works more like Google Maps , providing far more complete data on the butterflies ' locations to anyone , almost in real time . <p> Knowing more about butterflies ' individual habits may enable us to help them thrive collectively ( more on that later ) . The data also just help us get to know the monarchs better . As it turns out , some seem to be worse with directions than others , and can find themselves traveling west or south from Mexico . In the prototyping stage , some monarchs were detected near oil platforms in the Gulf of Mexico , nowhere near land , and some butterflies appeared @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ follow well-established paths along invisible corridors in the air . <p> Cinderella , Eric , and Carle had all been tagged in Sierra Chincua , which meant that I could deduce certain facts about their lives . They were already bona fide world-class wanderers , having flown there all the way from Canada in the fall , a three-month trip . Along the way , they 'd dodged birds and windmills and cars , eventually reaching the same patch of forest -- perhaps even the same trees -- that their great-grandparents had roosted in . For much of the winter , they had not eaten and had barely moved , jamming themselves so tightly against their fellow travelers that from a distance they looked like scales armoring the sides of the trees . Now they 'd awakened , stretching their wings and warming up . <p> Ana Hop for The Atlantic <p> Monarchs at rest on tree branches at El Rosario in early March . In winter 's colder temperatures , the butterflies pack themselves together so tightly that they resemble scales on the trees . <p> Though I could @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from my phone that Cinderella , Eric , and Carle were near . Over the next several weeks , I would keep tabs on them , following their movements with a level of detail that would have been impossible only a year ago . <p> Individual butterflies can travel as far as 100 miles in a single day and can congregate in groups so thick that they show up as clouds on weather radar . Glider pilots have seen them flying at altitudes above 10,000 feet . Year after year , new generations tend to gather around the same landmarks , despite having never seen them before . They seem , for example , to love the Barnegat Lighthouse on Long Beach Island , in New Jersey . <p> The monarchs ' feats of navigation are enabled by their internal technology , a set of instinctual tools that in some ways surpasses our understanding . The butterflies ' eyes can sense the angle and intensity of different forms of light . In each antenna , a complex series of molecules maintains their circadian clock ? , which , when combined @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> When the sun is unreliable , monarchs have a secondary method of pathfinding -- a biological compass that allows them to sense Earth 's magnetic field . Internal thermostats help govern the timing of their northward movements . <p> The butterflies ' migration evolved to fit North America 's geography . Monarch caterpillars eat only milkweed , which grows best in cooler climates , but the butterflies ca n't tolerate freezing temperatures . The length of the continent 's interior , unbroken by mountains or oceans , allows the monarchs to find the right weather and plants throughout the year -- a Goldilocks ecology , neither too hot nor too cold . They are a marvel of nature 's engineering , of the kind of elegance that evolution can achieve over deep time . <p> " Winter is hard for insects that have to survive it , and monarchs basically avoid it by leaving , " says Karen Oberhauser , a researcher at the University of Wisconsin at Madison and a co-founder of the Monarch Butterfly Fund . Unlike many other butterfly species , which are adapted to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ environment changes . <p> Indeed , they seem strikingly well adapted for living alongside the world 's most disruptive change agent : humanity . Milkweed tends to thrive in transitional areas , such as fallow fields , unkempt lawns , and the edges of highways . " When I first started studying monarchs , it really did seem like they kind of had an advantage over a lot of other species , " Oberhauser told me . " That 's a disturbance-adapted species . " She said , affectionately , that monarchs are like rats or pigeons , all part of the strange but sturdy menagerie of modern life . <p> But there are limits to any creature 's adaptability , and human activity has steadily eroded the monarch 's margin for error . In the 1930s , Mexico 's land-reform program created ejidos , areas designated for communal use by peasants . They began clearing the ancient oyamel forests to plant corn , beans , and squash , and sold the felled timber . Commercial interests also stepped in , accelerating logging in the butterfly 's winter habitats . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ disturbances to the disturbances . Although milkweed can flourish along roads , the advent of the interstate system also introduced one of the most efficient killers of butterflies : speeding traffic . And although farmland in the Midwest was once a reliable ( if accidental ) incubator for milkweed , the development of Roundup Ready crops in the ' 90s allowed more promiscuous use of herbicides . Almost overnight , milkweed disappeared from large-scale farms . <p> According to a recent report from the World Wildlife Fund , the total area covered by monarch colonies in winter decreased from 45 acres in 1996 to a low of fewer than two acres in 2013 . The five-year average of colony size has dropped by 80 percent since the mid- ' 90s , a plunge of some 170 million butterflies . <p> As Oberhauser told me , the changes wrought by humans have caught up to the monarchs . In fact , the complexity of the migration might now be a liability , as multiple stops on the journey face upheaval at once . " What makes them so strong also makes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of butterflies had been strong from their beginnings . As caterpillars , they 'd gorged on milkweed , a plant that is toxic to most animals , and incorporated its poison into their bodies . Their distinct coloring is both a warning for predators and a badge of initiation . <p> They are already one-percenters -- only one in 100 of the eggs laid in Ontario last year had produced butterflies that made it this far . Their generation , called a " super-generation , " is bigger and more robust than their parents ' was or their children 's will be : They are centurions built to make the journey all the way south and partway back . ( Two successive generations will eventually complete the round trip to Canada . ) Now , after a winter of fasting , huddled close against the cold , they are regaining their strength , sipping nectar from flowers near their roosting trees . On my phone , I can see a map with each location that the butterflies ping as they meander from place to place , ever returning to their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , not their more private matters , but I know that before they leave these forests behind , they will each find a mate . For Cinderella and Eric -- which , as I told my daughter , is a female butterfly -- their mates ' package of sperm will also contain a deposit of nutrients that will help them handle the rigors of the trip . Carle will weaken himself , giving his mate a gift that she must use to ensure the survival of their offspring . <p> For the past several decades , a diverse group of grassroots activists , scientists , and enthusiasts has worked to forestall the monarchs ' decline . I 'd come to Sierra Chincua as the guest of one of them . Jos ? Luis ? lvarez Alcal ? , a local activist and farmer , showed me around . The growers would still be free to profit from their land by selling timber for Christmas trees , lumber , or charcoal , but would be encouraged to harvest the wood sustainably . If done right , on a wide-enough scale , the model could help provide both income for the ejidatarios and the basis for butterfly tourism . <p> ? lvarez Alcal ? says that , since launching in 1997 , his La Cruz Habitat Protection Project has planted about 20 million oyamel fir and pine trees . Other groups have also reforested hundreds of hectares . Climate change is an accelerant for troubles that are already afoot . High mountain elevations that were once cool and less hospitable for avocado growing are getting hotter -- and thus more tempting for felling . Warmer temperatures will also narrow the already tiny bands of habitability for oyamel firs , disrupting forest ecologies even in places that logging has n't yet touched . <p> The most ominous signs were visible from Hacienda la Cruz . Luca explained that these patches had been infested by pine bark beetles , notorious evergreen-killers that feed on stressed or weakened conifers . The beetles are a normal part of forest ecosystems , and their numbers are usually controlled by cold winters . But the season has grown warmer . More trees now suffer from heat stress and dehydration , making them targets . Oyamels are particularly vulnerable . Carle is more of a homebody , sticking closer to Sierra Chincua and often staying in its forests . <p> Eric is the first of the trio to leave for good , but her eagerness might be her undoing -- the weather might not yet be warm enough for a successful transit . " <p> In the United States and Canada , the math leads to a similar place as it does in Mexico . For all the bootstrapped recent successes in forestalling the butterfly 's decline , we 've merely been playing catch-up . Now climate change is the primary threat to monarch numbers , and it is implacable . Higher temperatures make monarchs more susceptible to infection by a protozoan called Ophryocystis elektroscirrha , a parasite that drains the butterfly 's strength and makes it less likely to complete its journeys . And climate shifts are yet another threat to milkweed 's viability in particular locales , throwing off the distances and timing that are so crucial to each butterfly 's travels . <p> " Maybe we can do something about the climate , but the opportunities for doing that are really , really diminishing very rapidly , " Taylor said . Even accounting for this year 's @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 20 years or so before the migrating monarch 's numbers become too low to recover . The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service estimates upwards of a 50 percent chance that the eastern migratory monarchs will be extinct by 2080 . <p> When I was growing up , the flowers outside my bedroom window always brought visitors . I 'd often wake up in the morning to the buzzing of a hummingbird 's wings . But the visitors I greeted most joyfully were the monarchs . On late-summer days , kaleidoscopes of butterflies would land together , covering the flowers like an orange blanket . Even then , I understood that there was something special about their fleeting delicateness . And fleeting it was : The world will probably never again see as many monarchs as existed then . <p> But I would not bet entirely against the monarch . The fight to protect the species continues . In its persistence and its cooperative nature , that fight shares qualities with the monarch itself . The project is proof , in a fractured time , that people can join together and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> Even as Taylor despaired of the monarch 's future , he mused , briefly , about the possibility of restocking wild-butterfly populations using a scaled-up version of Monarch Watch 's colony program . With that , his exertions in perfecting his homegrown colony process -- the green smoothie for caterpillars , testing procedures for parasites -- came into clearer view for me . They could be understood , I realized , as the beginnings of an attempt to bottle a fragment of the monarch 's magic for the future world . Maybe migration will continue , in that future , with an assist from something like a butterfly doomsday vault . Or maybe the next generation of the monarch 's friends will find further innovations to defeat problems that now seem insurmountable . <p> The things we have learned recently about individual butterflies will be helpful for vanquishing those scientific problems , but the most important contribution of all this new information might be wonder itself . I am not a scientist , but after watching and tracking the butterflies for months , it seems to me that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ individuality that might endear them even more to the public . Some appear impulsive , or impressively rangy , leaving earlier and traveling more , whereas others fail to leave altogether . In El Rosario , when I asked Silvestre , my guide , about why groups of butterflies chose specific times to find water , he was enigmatic : " They decide . " <p> And whether or not we call it personality , there 's science behind the idea of the butterflies ' individuality . In humans , traits such as neuroticism and recklessness appear to have some evolutionary basis -- emerging perhaps to provide our species with sentries who alerted us to threats , and with explorers who led the way to new food sources . Research suggests that the goofball butterflies that might seem to lack a sense of direction are actually pioneers , scouts finding new habitats to exploit or new places to hunker down should worse come to worst . <p> No matter what happens along the Mexico route , the monarch is likely to live on , albeit in smaller numbers , elsewhere @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ -- in Spain , in Hawaii -- are likely descended from vagrants that veered off the Mexico migration path . Monarchs introduced to Australia in the 1870s have now established an annual migration along the east and southwest coasts -- they have earned the fitting local epithet of " wanderers . " And in little pockets of the United States , Central America , and the Caribbean , in places where milkweed grows year-round , small monarch populations have settled in , no longer migrating and now sporting shorter wingspans than their more transient cousins . <p> For all their seeming fragility , monarchs will be an extraordinarily capable partner in efforts to save them . They may yet surprise us . We may yet surprise ourselves . <p> Cinderella is a marathoner . She flies through San Antonio and then skirts Austin , avoiding the congestion of the city proper during the South by Southwest festival , and then cuts through the Piney Woods toward Texarkana . At the time of her last detection , Cinderella is in Arkansas , having likely traveled from the Great Lakes to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ she belongs to has spread out across a wide expanse of the country , ending up in Florida , Georgia , Mississippi , Louisiana , Oklahoma , and every place in between . They 've spent themselves , but they have fulfilled their biological missions . <p> During her roughly eight-month life , Cinderella traveled farther than scientists might have imagined possible , more than 3,000 miles as the butterfly flies . Along the way , she likely glued hundreds of tiny eggs onto milkweed plants . Perhaps some of the hatchlings will live , and continue her line . Perhaps they , too , will have her preternatural strength and tenacity . I hope , for the sake of my own daughter , and her ability to live in a world of beauty , that they will . <p> This article appears in the September 2026 print edition with the headline " How Do Monarchs Do It ? @@139360160 <h> Storms born of flame : What ' fire clouds ' are , and why Europe 's worst wildfires in decades may burn for weeks <h> A fire-born thunderstorm is driving Europe 's worst wildfires in decades , and a heatwave is still to come . <p> Wildfires of a scale not seen in living memory are burning through southwestern France and central Spain , and have together forced more than 300,000 people from their homes . <p> Wildfires in France and Spain have forced more than 300,000 people to flee their homes . <p> In France 's Gironde region , a blaze that erupted near Saumos on July 22 has consumed more than 420 sq km of forest and scrubland -- an area roughly four times the size of Paris -- damaged or destroyed more than 240 homes , injured 80 firefighters , and come within 15km of the outskirts of Bordeaux . <p> In Spain , separate fires burning west and south of Madrid have scorched about 770 sqkm , an area larger than the capital itself . The fire in ? vila province , at roughly 500 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , the government has said . <p> French President Emmanuel Macron , visiting a crisis coordination centre near Bordeaux on Monday , called the situation France 's worst fire-related crisis since World War II . " We 're facing a completely unprecedented fire , " he said . <p> While calmer conditions on Monday allowed crews to halt the main fire 's advance , nobody is declaring victory yet . A fresh heatwave arrives in France on Tuesday and in Spain by Wednesday , threatening to undo the gains . " The weeks ahead will be hard . We must hold firm , " Macron said . <p> Part of what has made these fires so destructive , and so hard to predict , is a phenomenon France has never recorded before : the fire generating its own thunderstorm . <p> Beach goers sit on the beach of Moutchic in Lacanau , southwestern France , as clouds of smoke rise into the sky from a wildfire . ( AFP ) A French Civil Security Canadair CL-415 aircraft flies past flames burning a forest surrounding Cotignac , southeastern France , during @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cloud ' over Gironde <p> On Friday evening , two days after the Gironde fire began , the departmental fire and rescue service confirmed that the blaze had produced a pyrocumulonimbus -- a " fire cloud " , or pyroCb -- a vast , electrified storm cloud created by the fire itself . <p> France 's national firefighters federation said the country had never documented one before . <p> The cloud generated lightning that struck the ground beyond the fire 's edge and ignited fresh blazes , along with violent , erratic winds that drove the flames in new directions . The Gironde fire chief , briefing Macron , described a fire unlike any his crews had tackled -- " a fire that 's going everywhere " . <p> The cloud weakened overnight as humidity rose , then re-formed several times over subsequent days . In effect , the fire began making its own weather . <h> The science of the cloud <p> The mechanics are those of an ordinary thunderstorm , but one supercharged by flames . <p> " Imagine a campfire so large and hot that the smoke @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Theodore M Giannaros , a fire meteorologist at the National Observatory of Athens , told AP . " That is a pyroCb -- a storm cloud born from the intense heat that a wildfire produces . " <p> A sufficiently intense fire sends hot air , smoke and moisture surging upwards in a plume . As the plume climbs , it cools . Water vapour condenses around particles of ash , and once above the freezing level , droplets become ice crystals . Their collisions separate electrical charges , which can then cause lightning . <p> Giannaros said there 's no set temperature at which these clouds form . What they need is very hot , very dry air near the ground and cooler , moister air above . <p> These clouds are enormous and can reach altitudes of 10-15km and punch into the stratosphere . <p> Nasa has called them the " fire-breathing dragon of clouds " , The Guardian reported . <p> HT Graphic <h> Why they make everything worse <p> Once formed , a pyroCb can amplify the disaster that created it . <p> Rising air pulls @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ back to the surface . Those shifts can turn the flames , split them into several fronts , or send them racing in a new direction . <p> Lightning can also spark spot fires far beyond the main blaze , and the systems can even spin up whirling columns of flame . <p> " It 's a feedback loop , not a one-way effect , " Giannaros told AP . " Once it forms , the cloud becomes its own weather system , sitting on top of the fire and making it harder to predict . " <p> For firefighters , escape routes can close without warning as fresh outbreaks appear behind crews and equipment , sometimes forcing them to abandon direct attack and fall back to defend towns . The gravest danger , Giannaros said , is fire moving faster than evacuations can be organised . <p> PyroCbs have until now been essentially an Australian and North American phenomenon -- Canada alone produced a record 142 in 2023 . <p> During Australia 's 2019-2020 summer , fire officials in New South Wales reported embers from a pyroCb landing 30km @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ That season , 33 people , including four firefighters , were killed , and more than 11 million hectares of bush , forest and parkland was burnt . <p> Similar storms have been reported above California and neighbouring American states in 2021 . <p> In Europe pyroCbs are uncommon . Portugal recorded one during its deadly 2017 fire season , and France has now joined the list . <p> TOPSHOT - This handout photo released on July 26 , 2026 and taken on July 24 , 2026 by Spain 's Military Emergencies Unit ( UME ) shows UME personnel fighting a wildfire near Navas del Rey , 55 kilometers southwest of Madrid . Spain faces two crucial days to bring raging wildfires under control before weather conditions worsen , authorities said on July 27 , 2026 , though they were making some progress and planned to start returning evacuees to certain areas . The wildfires that broke out in forested areas around the Spanish capital at the height of the summer holidays have forced some 60,000 people to flee their homes , often with just minutes ' notice , leaving @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ UME / AFP ) / RESTRICTED TO EDITORIAL USE - MANDATORY CREDIT " AFP PHOTO / SPAIN 'S MILITARY EMERGENCIES UNIT ( UME ) / HANDOUT " - NO MARKETING NO ADVERTISING CAMPAIGNS - DISTRIBUTED AS A SERVICE TO CLIENTS ( AFP ) Combining data from the European Forest Fire Information System ( Effis ) and NASA Visible Infrared Imaging Radiometer Suite ( VIIRS ) shows a map depicting active wildfires , the progression of burned areas , and the distance of the active fires from the city of Bordeaux , southwestern France . ( AFP ) <h> What is driving the wildfires <p> The immediate fuel for the Spain and France wildfires is a summer of successive heatwaves that arrived early and hit hard , drying out forests and scrubland across western Europe , with little rain since May . <p> Human activity supplies most of the sparks . French Prime Minister S ? bastien Lecornu wrote on X that nine out of 10 fires are human-caused , most through carelessness -- a cigarette butt , a barbecue , work carried out without precaution , Reuters reported . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fastest-warming continent , heating at roughly twice the global average since the 1980s , according to the EU 's Copernicus Climate Change Service and the World Meteorological Organization . <p> In both Bordeaux and ? vila , among the worst struck areas , July 's high temperatures have averaged 32 ? C , almost 6 degrees above the 1961-1990 norm , the Reuters Climate Monitor reported . <p> Spanish officials have also classed some of the blazes as " sixth-generation " fires , the most extreme and uncontrollable category . <p> " What we 're experiencing is n't a succession of isolated events . It 's the most painful expression of a climate emergency that is making sixth-generation wildfires more ferocious , heatwaves more frequent , and our territory more vulnerable , " Spanish Prime Minister Pedro S ? nchez said . <p> An aerial view of scorched ground in the Camino de Navahonda area , following a wildfire near Robledo de Chavela , 60 kilometers west of Madrid . ( AFP ) Smoke emanating from the wildfire north of the Arcachon basin covering the forest , with a view @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ southwestern France , ( AFP ) <h> The threat ahead : a fourth heatwave <p> Officials have said the wildfires will likely burn for many weeks , and some could take months to fully extinguish . <p> Adding to that devastation is a looming heatwave . <p> France 's weather office forecasts temperatures of 35 ? C and above in the southwest from Tuesday , rising towards 40 ? C with dry winds . Spain 's weather agency AEMET has announced the country 's fourth heatwave of the year from Wednesday , with temperatures of up to 42 ? C in the northeast , warning that fire danger will climb to extreme levels on high heat , gusty southerly winds and widespread low humidity . <p> " Today and tomorrow are absolutely crucial days in tackling this wildfire . From Wednesday onwards , weather forecasts become significantly more unfavourable , " Spanish interior minister Fernando Grande-Marlaska was quoted as saying by Reuters . <p> Scientists say hotter , drier conditions give extreme fires more opportunity to spawn fire clouds and such storms , though long-term data is still too thin @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @

@@139365476 <p> * In France : The heat is likely to make already dangerous fire conditions much worse and face masks have been issued in areas affected by air pollution . The fire in the southwest Gironde region remained stable overnight , local authorities said , as military engineers battle flames alongside firefighters . <p> Pilots hover over a water source , lower the bucket to fill it , then fly to the fire and release the water , usually directed by crews on the ground . A Blackhawk 's bucket typically holds 600 to 700 gallons of water , according to the US-based United Aerial Firefighters Association ( UAFA ) -- and has been historically used to fight wildfires in places like California . Other types of helicopters used in Europe can carry about 2,600 gallons . <p> Helicopters can use lakes , rivers , reservoirs or large ponds -- bodies of water deep enough , and with enough surface area to allow the aircraft to safely hover and dip the bucket . But helicopters are limited in how fast they can travel when carrying water , which is why @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ aircraft are also used to fight wildfires . They 're amphibious planes that fly close to the surface of the water -- typically lakes or reservoirs , but sometimes rivers - scooping up water as they skim across . They can carry 700 to 1,600 gallons per load and need large bodies of water -- at least 5 feet deep , UAFA says . <p> " Typically with scoopers you want to be within 20 minutes of the fire , " says UAFA Executive Director Paul Peterson . <p> Water-dropping aircraft are just part of the aerial response . Fixed-wing airtankers carry thousands of gallons of fire retardant -- a usually bright-colored substance that coats vegetation and slows the spread of flames , or water mixed with chemical enhancers . These aircraft are typically loaded at airports or airbases before carrying out their mission . <p> But key to all these operations is safety . Aircraft need clear , unobstructed access to water sources , which means the public -- especially boaters and swimmers - should stay away during operations , or move away quickly if they see such an @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which includes a five-star hotel -- has n't received instructions to evacuate the premises just yet , but staff are preparing for the worst . <p> " We are on hold , " Fradin said . " It 's quite a scary and anxious situation , but we are ready to move if it is necessary and to try to save people and animals . " <p> Fradin said that the Ch ? teau Smith Haut Lafitte has developed a plan in case staff need to move their livestock , which includes cows and llamas . In the meantime , the winery is providing firefighters with pastries and water . <p> The fire 's proximity to France 's famous vineyards near Bordeaux has caused alarm throughout the wine-loving world . Ch ? teau Smith Haut Lafitte produces some of the most highly regarded - and expensive - wines . <p> Forest fires often cause ripe grapes to take on a bitter taste from smoke , an issue known as " smoke taint " in the wine industry . On Saturday , wine expert Nicolle Croft said that she 'd received @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 2026 vintage . <p> However , Croft told CNN that the grapes around Bordeaux are n't yet ripe enough to be harmed by smoke . " The grapes are like very hard , small peas , " she said . " They are n't absorbing any aromas . " <p> However , if the fires continue for longer , the smoke may become a problem . Dr. Phil Crews , a winemaker and chemist at the University of California , Santa Cruz , told CNN on Saturday that as grapes ripen , their sugars could bind with phenols in smoke . The resulting taint is " ashy , foul tasting and smelling , " Crews said . <p> " If the wind is blowing in the direction of the vineyard , then there 's going to be a problem , " said Crews . <p> Link Copied ! <h> 24,000 evacuees in Spain allowed to return home <p> By Pau Mosquera <p> About 24,000 of the 63,000 people who had been evacuated amid the wildfires in Spain have been allowed to return home , according to Francisco Mart ? n @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Lockdown restrictions were eased on another 20,000 people in the region . <p> " Right now , we are in the process of bringing the wildfire under control , " Mart ? n said . @@139368366 <p> Israelis viewed social cohesion and national security as the issues facing the greatest threats , according to a new report published by the Institute for National Security Studies ( INSS ) on Monday . <p> The report is based on three surveys conducted in February and July . <p> The first , conducted in February , surveyed 921 members of the general public . A second survey , carried out in July , collected responses from 968 members of the general public . The third , also conducted in February , examined the perceptions of 147 experts from research , academia , the defense industry , and the government sector . <p> Each survey was weighted to match Israel 's population , with 20% of respondents coming from the Arab community . <p> Respondents were asked to select the three most threatening scenarios facing Israel in the next five years , from a list of 14 scenarios . <p> The results published in July showed that an equal proportion of Israelis ( 39% ) viewed both violent conflict between different social groups and the possibility of Iran acquiring a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , there was a slight decline in the number of respondents ranking these issues as the most threatening compared with February , when 42% expressed concern over potential internal violence and 40% over Iran 's nuclear ambitions . <p> A person holds a placard representing a US flag , with an image of Iran 's new Supreme Leader Mojtaba Khamenei on a billboard in the background , on the day of a ceremony marking 40 days since Ayatollah Ali Khamenei was killed , in Tehran , Iran , April 9 , 2026. ( credit : MAJID ASGARIPOUR/WANA ( WEST ASIA NEWS AGENCY ) VIA REUTERS ) <p> Fewer Israelis were also concerned about the prospect of a large-scale missile attack on the home front , with the figure declining from 38% in February to 35% in July . Similarly , concern that the state 's democratic character could be undermined fell from 34% in February to 31% in July . <p> In contrast , there appeared to be a growing fear of a prolonged war on one of Israel 's fronts , with 27% ranking this as among their top @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> More Israelis are also worried about international isolation and sanctions , with the figure rising 5% to 26% in the latest survey . <p> The public 's concerns were felt even more strongly among experts , who identified the undermining of the state 's democratic character ( 67% ) , violent conflict between societal groups ( 58% ) , and international isolation and sanctions ( 44% ) as the most significant threats . <p> Across the board , few rated climate change as a significant cause of concern . @@139370867 <p> New Yorkers understand that climate change is an existential threat . And they also understand that there is no time to waste when it comes to the kind of bold , game changing actions that will be required to mitigate its most harmful effects . That is why New York 's governor and Legislature passed groundbreaking legislation -- the Climate Change Superfund Act -- to hold the largest polluters accountable for the impacts they have on our health and wellbeing . <p> But now , extremists in Congress are working hand in hand with Washington lobbyists in an attempt to block New York State 's landmark law . New Yorkers should not allow Washington extremists to tell us what we can and can not do to protect our environment and address the harmful effects of pollution before time runs out . <p> In 2024 , state lawmakers passed the legislation to make polluters pay their fair share and contribute $3 billion per year to cover some of the cost of protecting ourselves from future extreme weather events . These funds will help us prepare for and respond to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ temperatures -- from improving and repairing roads , bridges , buildings , drainage , and more . <p> It 's a proactive approach that shifts at least some of the burden from taxpayers onto those most responsible for the problem : massive and wildly profitable oil and gas giants , like ExxonMobil and Saudi Aramco . <p> For New York residents and businesses , this is n't just an abstract concept . Extreme weather events lead to higher taxes , insurance rates , and utility bills due to damaged buildings and infrastructure . <p> New Yorkers already know how hard it is to cover their utility bills , which have spiked drastically over the past year , and how expensive it is to repeatedly repair damage to their homes from flood after flood . Our climate Superfund law mitigates these financial pressures , providing New Yorkers a small buffer against the economic fallout . <p> Some estimates put the price of protecting New York from the impacts of climate change as high as $52 billion to protect New York Harbor , $75-$100 billion to protect Long Island , and $55 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ comptroller predicted that more than half of local governments ' costs will be attributable to the climate crisis . <p> Until now , these costs have fallen on taxpayers , even though Big Oil -- which has seen booming business ( even bigger now due to the Iran crisis ) and record profits -- has accelerated the pace of climate change and has never been held accountable . <p> Importantly , the climate Superfund law does n't make Big Oil pay for all of the costs -- just a small fraction . But this is a significant improvement from forcing taxpayers to front the entire bill . The math is simple : If polluters do n't pay , you and other taxpayers will . <p> Republicans and Democrats alike need to recognize that climate change is here , and it is not going away . Protecting our state while lowering costs for taxpayers should not be a controversial idea . And most importantly -- New York should be able to set its own laws without interference from politicians beholden to the fossil fuel industry . <p> After Superstorm Sandy , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ disaster assistance to our state . It was a bipartisan team effort that could not lose . <p> Copyright 2026 New York Daily News . All rights reserved . @@139400070 <h> A new study found that while heat affects visits to Southern Europe 's heritage sites , tourists keep coming and adapt without reducing satisfaction , <p> SouthernEurope is a popular tourism hub that in recent years has also become a climate change " hotspot , " experiencing a warming rate 20% higher than the global average alongside frequent and intense heatwaves . This makes the peak tourism season at many popular sites hot in every sense . <p> A new study by Dr. Eran Ketter , a tourism consultant and researcher from the Department of Tourism and Hotel Management at Kinneret Academic College , and Dotan Farkash , sought to examine the " authentic " field experience of tourists and understand how the heat alters the visiting experience at open heritage sites ( those situated under the open sky ) . Through an analysis of TripAdvisor reviews from 11 open heritage sites in Southern Europe ( such as the Colosseum in Rome and the Acropolis in Athens ) , the researchers examined how the climate crisis affects the way we consume tourism , and what adaptations are required of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ heat , but satisfied with the visit <p> Ketter explains that the choice of heritage sites was deliberate . " Fewer external interventions are made at heritage sites because people want to preserve their authenticity , so it is unlikely they will add interventions like large pergolas and air conditioners , " he says . The study examined 6,500 reviews published on TripAdvisor during the warm half of the year - April to September - over a four-year period . The reviews were cross-referenced with weather data from the time of the visit , including meteorological measurements from the nearest monitoring stations to each site , average monthly temperatures , the number of days per month above the average temperature , humidity data , and heat stress levels . " We conducted a correlation analysis between the reviews and the data ; we checked the frequency of expressions related to heat or relevant infrastructure - for example , words like ' shade , ' ' bench , ' and ' water station ' - in the months that were hotter , " Ketter says . <p> Indeed , the study @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on exceptionally hot days with those made on relatively cool days during the six-month warm period . It found that the word " heat " and descriptions of " thermal intensity " ( such as exhausting heat or a feeling of " stickiness " ) were significantly more common in reviews on hot days ( 2.36 times and 2.21 times more frequent , respectively ) . In addition , the reviews contained more references related to the expectation of infrastructure that helps cope with the heat , such as shade ( 2.15 times ) and drinking fountains ( 3.34 times ) . <p> However , despite the increase in reports of heat and the physical difficulty it brings , the study 's central and surprising finding is that the overall level of satisfaction ( the star rating ) emerging from the reviews was unaffected and remained high and stable . <p> The Colosseum , Rome . ( credit : Nofar Ronen ) <h> More water and less walking <p> Ketter explains the discrepancy : " It appears we have a certain adaptation of visitor expectations here , " he says @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ hot , but they still want to visit the Acropolis , and they understand that this heat is part of the visit . So it is hot and people complain , but still - the site is an important and amazing heritage site . " <p> He views this insight as positive and optimistic for the future of tourism . " It truly shows resilience , because it is not as if heatwaves in Europe mean tourists will stop visiting Spain , France , and Greece . They will continue to visit , and they will continue to visit in the summer , but they will also make micro-adaptations at the behavioral level and around the visiting experience , " he says . <p> An example of a micro-adaptation - meaning adjustments in visitor behavior and expectations - identified by the researchers is that at high temperatures , there is less engagement with walking around the site . Reviews written around hot days contained fewer mentions of walking paths at the site and visits to its distant sections . " On hot days , people apparently make their visit more @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to circle the Acropolis ; they want the minimum possible amount of walking , " Ketter says , emphasizing the active coping mechanisms of tourists who do not remain passive in the face ofclimate change , but rather adapt to the changing reality . " They have a vacation in August , and they will make the most of it . They will adapt both their expectations and their behavior - so they will take water and a hat and visit the Acropolis at 8 a.m. or at sunset , rather than in the middle of the day , but they will not give up on the vacation because of the heat , " he explains . <h> Adapting infrastructure to reality <p> Alongside the adaptation by tourists , tourism sites are also required to adapt to the changing reality . For this purpose , Ketter proposes a model based on three pillars : Tourism development that includes building adapted infrastructure and diversifying products and audiences ( such as transforming ski sites into summer leisure and music hubs ) ; the human element , which combines working with decision-makers so @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , alongside making practical knowledge accessible to entrepreneurs and field tourism personnel to build community resilience ; and finally , active crisis management , which includes formulating specific procedures for dealing with extreme events such as heatwaves , aiming to protect the health of visitors and staff and adjust the visiting experience in real time . <p> When it comes to openheritage sites , a unique challenge arises . Alongside the critical need to adapt infrastructure to the reality of the climate crisis and extreme heat conditions , there is a constraint due to the need to preserve the site 's authenticity . " It is not enough to just build a small pergola , " Ketter says . " These are sites that sometimes receive thousands of visitors a day . There needs to be an adjustment of walking paths and real shade that will actually allow people to sit comfortably in the shade for a few minutes every 400 to 500 meters . " The solution , according to him , should include natural shade through vegetation , shorter shaded routes , and technology that monitors heat stress @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to real-time weather conditions . <p> Tourists during the recent heatwave in Athens . ( credit : REUTERS ) <p> " The study teaches us that tourists are already adapting , but site managers are required to do so as well , " Ketter concludes . " Every site manager must ask themselves how the climate crisis affects the visiting experience at their site , and act proactively to adapt the visitor experience to the constraints of the climate crisis . " <p> The article was prepared byZAVIT -- the news agency of the Israel Society of Ecology and Environmental Sciences . 
@@139402461 <p> New road map for Democrats says working-class voters key to futureWorking-class voters have moved away from the Democratic Party . Winning them back is key to the party 's future , a new reports shows . But first , the messaging has to change . <p> People vote at a polling location in New York City on Nov. 4 , 2025 . Leonardo Munoz/AFP via Getty Images hide caption <p> toggle caption <p> Leonardo Munoz/AFP via Getty Images <p> There 's an active debate in Democratic politics between progressives and moderates about how to build a winning coalition . <p> A new report from a pair of California researchers , shared exclusively with NPR , argues that debate misses the point . The answer is n't centrism , they write , it 's respect for where working-class voters are coming from . <p> " It 's about understanding that people need a sense of personal and economic security before they will support the changes progressives are calling for , " the authors write . <p> The report 's authors are , to put a label on it , college-educated @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ from San Francisco who is known for her work in diversity , equity and inclusion . Jared Abbott leads the Center for Working Class Politics and has a Ph.D . from Harvard . Their intended audience is people who share their progressive politics . <p> Sponsor Message <p> Their message is that the Democratic Party would be making a huge mistake if it keeps catering to social justice warriors and fails to understand , respect and appeal to working-class voters . " The path heavily favors the party that wins working-class voters , " the report explains . <p> " I am tired of losing , " Williams said in an interview . " I want to build a sustained progressive coalition capable of delivering on progressive goals . And that 's not what we have now , and that 's not what we 've had for decades . " <p> Titled " The Left 's Coalition Crisis : Understanding the Massive Divide Between Working Class and College Educated Voters , and How to Bridge It , " the report argues that Democrats have lost support from working-class voters of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ touch with the priorities and values of those Americans . <p> " Until Democrats understand the importance of really respecting , listening to and taking seriously the values of self-reliance , the values of patriotism and tradition and personal security , " Abbott said , " they 're going to have a major messaging and a major electoral problem . " <p> Abbott and Williams define the working class as people without a four-year college degree , earning from $40,000 to $100,000 a year -- the middle 50% of American incomes . <p> " If progressives want to build working-class support , they need to connect with people whose top priorities are paychecks , prices and personal safety , " the report states . <p> The authors offer a two-part prescription to " develop the cultural competence to bridge the diploma divide . " <p> " First , consistently embrace a populism that decries a rigged economy where hard work fails to yield a stable middle class life , " it states . <p> The report relies on the results from the Class Gaps Poll , a survey of more @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ finds there 's a disconnect between the priorities of college-educated liberals and those of working-class voters on issues like LGBTQ+ rights , abortion access , climate change , immigration and income redistribution . <p> Loading ... <p> The greatest divide is on the issue of " resisting authoritarianism . " <p> " Resisting authoritarianism is the second-highest priority for liberal college grads , but for workers , it 's like 19 out of 20 , " said Williams . <p> Defending democracy was a major theme of Vice President Kamala Harris ' losing presidential campaign in 2024 . She delivered her closing argument from the White House Ellipse , recounting President Trump 's actions on Jan. 6 , 2021 . <p> Another major theme of her campaign was defending abortion access . Those are two issues , according to the report , " that liberal college grads prioritize but most working-class Americans do n't . " <p> The lesson , they argue , is n't that working-class voters are unreachable . It 's that you ca n't reach them if you 're not focused on the issues they care about @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ stopping authoritarianism , if we really care about defending democracy , we have to stop talking about defending democracy in the way that we 've been doing for the last too many years , " Abbott said . <p> So , what should Democrats be talking about ? The economy , and not just in abstract terms like " affordability , " because wages are as important as costs , the researchers find . <p> " Ten out of 12 workers ' top priorities are economics , so if you want them to vote for you , talk about economics , " Williams said . <p> Abbott said Democratic candidates this midterm election year have done a relatively good job of keeping that economic focus . But how they talk about economic issues matters too . <p> The researchers took issues that were n't top priorities for working-class voters and poll-tested new messages framed around working-class values . That shift in messaging was able to move opinion . <p> Sponsor Message <p> " If you care passionately about climate change , and I do , the answer is to tie @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Williams said . <p> When they framed climate initiatives around creating jobs , addressing rising energy costs , reducing pollution and countering China , they got much more support from working-class voters than asking if they support " major investments in energy efficiency and cleaner power to reduce pollution and address the climate crisis even if this raises the cost of energy a little . " <p> But , the paper warns , working-class reframing only works if it comes from a genuine place of trying to reach across the class divide , or it risks being seen as elites trying to manipulate non-elites . <p> Notably , Abbott says , reframing most issues did n't drive down support from college-educated liberals . <p> " In general , we found that there are no clear systematic tradeoffs , that what 's better messaging for the working class is typically better messaging for all voters , " Abbott said .
